_ The Japanese steamer Moko Maru, 7,144 tons, sank 
yesterday afternoon in heavy weather in the Gulf of 
Lingaven, in the Philippine Islands, but her entire crew, 
was saved by the Norwegian steamer Concordia, -

yesterday, had been variously reported damaged and 
chased off her course by'a typhoon. It now appears 
‘her delay was caused by an attempt to reach the Moko 
Maru. The Anking carried 425 passengers for Manila, 
mostly from Amoy. ; ig 

At noon yesterday the Moko Maru, bound from 
Moji to Dungan, reported she was making water in her 
forward holds but that she was in no need of immediate 
assistance. A half an hour later, however, she ‘sent 


an SOS crackling across the water, adding that she was 
taking great quantities of water and was unable to cope 
- with the situation unaided. She was then in latitude

comment on the speech delivered 
yesterday by King Leopold, in which

future maintain a strictly neutral

recognising her International engaye- 
ments. Consequently she docs not 
envisage withdrawing from: the 


League.’

the League. © : .

TRACED TO 1812 


Berkeley, Cal., Oct. 10. 

The civil war in-Spain will not 
precipitate a gencral European 
wor, ‘says Maj. Gen. David 
Prescott Barrows, professor of 
political science at the University 
of Californin and'farmer pre- 
aident of that institution. 

Dr. Barrows, an authority on 
European tendencies, discredits 
the idea that the Spanish 
struggle ennnot be localised and 
must provoke a general Euro- 
pean conflict. 

“This comes," he says, “from an 
exaggerated and unwarranted view 
of. that internationalism = which 
teaches that a war anywhere 1s 
everybody's war. 

“Dread of « Hed Spain is behind 
the present rebellion but the group 
thatas balling: the government prob- 
ably 1s not Fascist in the Italinn or 
German sense. Instead, it ts made 
up of many elements which have no 
common political philosophy, but are 
merely held together by the one 
common drend of a Red Spain, 

SHARP SWING TO LEFT 

Dr. Barrows emphnsizes that this 
has come about because the govern- 
ment in Spain of to-day ‘has passed 
from ‘conservative hands to u group 


“Its tendency,” he says, “has been 
to swing more and more toward an 
extreme revolutionary position, ever 
since coming into power In 1931. ~ 

“However, the present struggle has 
n deep, historical background and 1s 
not merely a rebellion by irresponsi- 
ble factions. It is not a cnsual 
struggic between forces without

.ONE PARTY UNCHANGED 

According to Dr. Barrows, the one

‘LABOUR’S 
PLAN TOEND 
THE LORDS 


SINGLE-CHAMBER 
GOVERNMENT 


By A Labour Correspondent 


For the first tlme in constitutional 
history an officint Opposition ts 
nbout ta adopt the question of the: 
nbolition of the House of Lords, and 
the establishment of a single-cham- 
ber Government, as a feature of Its 
programme, 


A report presented at the Soclal- 
ist Party Conference in Edinburgh, 
makes clear the intentions of the, 
Ieaders In this respect, 


This report, which was drawn up 
by the Constitutlonal Sub-Committee 
of the Policy Committee, has been 
adopted by the National Executive 
Committee. [t, therefore,. has the 
backing of ali the principal Soctalist 
leaders, 

There Is little doubt that the Can. 
ference will pass it, and thereby tic 
the Parliamentary Party to occep- 
tance und furtherance of the prin- 
ciples laid down. 


HONOURS 


One passage which appears under 
the sub-hending, “Honours,” reads: 
“So long us the House of Lords 
continues to exist, the Labour case 
must be competently presented 
there. Morcover, ft fs 
necessary for a certain number of 
Sceretarles and Under Secretaries 
of State to be members of the 
Upper. Mouse. Further, the crea- 
tion of: Peers in larxe numbers may 
prove to be the only possible way 
fo. abolish the Honse of Lords.” 


It is realised that no full-blooded 
Socialist programme fs likely to be 
attained within the lifetime of one 
Parliament, so long as two-Chamber 

—government- continues.—The-demand 
.for the abolition of the House of 
Lords ‘is thus for the first time given 
oficial colouring. 

Delegates at the conference will be 
reminded that: Ss 

Labour members, on appointment 
to the Cabinet, must actept member- 
‘ship of the Privy Councll. < 

The conferment of honours is a 


method of establishing status in the |’ 


Civil Service. 

Certain honours are conférred upon 
persons of distinction as recognition 
of merit and ‘service. 


The class of titles used in local 
government confers responsibility as 
well as distinction, 

The report adds:, “It would be 
impossible for the Labour Move- 
ment to lay down o Ddinding rule; 
which would bar Indlviduals from: 
accepiing- honours. A ruling of this 
kind could only be enforced by ex- 
pulsion from the Party, and if any 
honours are ta be recognised at all, 
the Mavement would be called upon 
to differentiate between the honours 
which ‘could nd could not be ac- 
cented-—a tasle which would not be 
without serious difficulties.” .

DIVORCE COLONY 
IN DUDE RANCH 
AREA VISIONED 


, Beulah, Wyo, Oct. 10. 

The dude ranch aren around Sand 
Creek, famous fishing stream fn the 
western edge of the Black Hills, may 
become a second Reno, if the plans 
of the Walpole’ Land Co., Ine. 
materialize. 

Since Wyoming has a divorce law 
requiring only 60 dnys residence, It 
has become popular as a divorce cens 

Mrs, Grace Vanderbilt Davis and 
Mra, Sylvia Conway Robertson, both 
of New York, recently won divorces 
after spending two months on dude 
ranches. : 

The plan of the land company is 

to erect a hotel, casino and numerous 
modern cottages, on Sand Creek, not 
far from the famous Moe Annenberg 
estate. Syndance, Wyo., a county 
seat town,-Is only a short distance 
away. There, it 1s said, divorces will 
be ‘obtained without accompanying 
publicity and fanfare. 
_ The Sand Creek district offers rex 
ercational facilities, fishing, hunting, 
horseback riding, mountain climbing 
and other outdoor sports. 


TOM THUMB RADIO 
SETS © 


VALVE NO BIGGER THAN 
AN ACORN 


'From A Radio Correspondent 


Tom Thumb wireless scts, no bigger 
than a cigarctte-case,-are likely to be 
on the mncket soon as a result of the 
development of a new midget valve. 

Known as the “Acorn” valve, be- 
cause of the similarity of its shape 
nnd size to the seed of the ok. it hag 
been produced by the Marconiphone 
Company for micro-waye work. 

It is extremely robust, and, al- 
though primarily for handling wave- 
lengths of the order of one metre, it 
Is equally efficient on. ordinary 
broadcasting wavelengths, ‘ 
_ At the moment its use Is likely to 
be limited owing to the price—50s.

“PLI- SHAFT” 
MODELS 


. During the past season 
_ we have been carrying out 
extensive ‘tests — with 
frames constructed on a 
still. more advanced 
‘principle, which is only 
made possible by. the - 
_ "FULCRUM" prodess of . 
building frames in block . 
form. Briefly, this new | 
frame has no handle slips 
at: all; . Every joint is . : 
parallel) to. the line of © am 
.. play, with the result that exceptional resitiency and 
extra drive “are obtained from the’ shaft. Under 
the normal construction the shaft is, of course, 
‘locked by the handle slips and the shock of impact 
is largely taken by the shoulders of the frames and, 
of course, the gut. Inour “Pii-shaft" construction . 
_ however, the shock is absorbed by the whole of tho 
_ shaft as well, with the result that:not only are the 
“shoulders (always the weakest part of the racket) - 
relleved of. some. of ‘the strain, but additional 
_ resiliency is-obtained as well.” Experience is rapidly 
leading us fo.conclude that the design of a racket 
’ along these ‘tines represents. one of the greatest 
advances which has ever. taken. place: in the | 
evolution of the tennis racket. i ‘

ng Gelegraph. 


The impatience felt in some 
quarters over the slowness of 
the international committee 
method, as applied to the non- 
intervention agreement in the 
Spanish crisis, raises an issue of 
the utmost importance. It is a 
matter which has = frequently 
come ‘into’ prominence in the 
troublous times of the past few, 
years in many parts of the 
world. The failure of. the 


League of Nations to prevent the , 


virtual absorption of Manchuria 
by Japan is. an’ outstanding 
example of the injury 2 country 
can suffer whilat an internation- 
al body is discussing the pros 
and cons of a crisis. Long 
before the Lytton Commission 
was despatched, Japan’s object 
had been achieved, and despite 
the fact that the Commissioners 
found that Japan’s action was 
indefensible, the stark fact re- 
mains that China has been bereft 
of un Immense-area: of-terrilory- 
which was indisputably hers. 
We saw a further instance of the 
in the Italo- 
Ethiopian dispute. Italy was 
declared ‘the, aggressor, . but 
whilst committee after commit- 
tee was brought into being at 
Geneva for the purposes of in- 
vestigation, and, painfully labori- 
ous machinery was set in 
motion, nothing intervened to 
prevent the dnnexation of a 
sovereign State ‘enjoying mem- 
bership of the . League of 
Nations. We are now witness- 
ing the same process in the 
cnse of the Spanish civil war. 
A most imposing list of nations 
hag agreed not to intervene in the 
conflict, but specific charges have 
been made that the agreement is 
not being observed. Under. the 


‘rules of procedure agreed upon, 


the Non-Intervention Committce 
will only listen to such charges 
as are submitted by 2 Govern- 
ment, which is " party to the 
agreement. It is then the duty 
of the Committee to examine the- 
complaints with.a view_ to as- 
certaining ‘the facts. In this 
inatance,-Russin, a signatory of 
the agreement, has _lovelled 
specific charges against Portugal 
and Germany, and these are now 
under process of investigation. 
The tragedy of the situation, so 
far as the. Spanish Government 
{s concerned, is that whilst the 
enquiries are being pursued, the 
insurgents, allegedly _ backed 
from the outside, ‘ure daily driv- 
ing on towards - the ‘capital. 
Whether one’s sympathies - are. 
with the loyalists -or the rebels, 
the. outstanding’ fact -is that 2. 
constitutionally established Gov- 
ernment is.in process. of being 
overthrown whilst. an” inter- 
national “body is engaged in 
seemingly endless talk to deter- 
mine whether there has been any 
outside ald givén to the rebels. 
On the .gencral issue. -ralsed, 
there fs accumulative evidence of

Cambridge, Mass., Oct. 12. 

The man who gave his donkey 

green glasses and sawdust, 
saving money only during the 
first week can try the experiment 
again with better hope of success. 
Dr. Friedrich Bergius, Heidelberg 
‘chemist, éxplaincd to Harvard 
tercentenary guests yesterday 
how food'can be synthesized from 
wood: | : . 

In fact, there fs enough. food 
in the waste scraps of a few 
forest acres-to feed inexpansive- 
dy the entire German army, he 
implied. Those who heard his 
address considered this to be a 
startling disclosure of the extent 
to which at least one‘nation has 
gone in the race toward nutional 
self-sufliciency. 

“During the Inst decades the world 

» haa become more than ever occupied

the nations within their: own fron+ 
tlers," he said, 


MINIMIZING DEPENDENCE -

. read. “Future armies will march on

WILL COST BUT LITTLE 

“Synthetic sawdust food will enst 
very little,” said Dr.’ Bergius, “for 
the raw materials are now waste 

‘ products. After the cost of the initial 
Plant installation, the upkeep will be 
small, All the ntaterials used {n the 
process are fully recoverable and can 
be used over ond over again. Even 
the fucl used for opernting the plant 
is obtuincd from n waste by-product 
of the sawdust, The final result fs 
w yield of 100 per cent.” 

Dr, Bergius said that at the pres= 
ert time the products of his plant 
are being used mostly as fodder for 
cattle, but-that at any time It cnn 
be converted into food fit: for ‘human 
consumption, Carbohydrates, fats

the duc date, is being considered by 
a Special Committee appointed by 
the Central Kuomintang. 


A proposal for the Congress to be 
postponed for three months only was 
considered: but ft is understood that 
this was ruled out. 


There Is also 1 body “of opinion | 


favouring holding the Congress on 
the scheduled dats, but it is_ pointed 
out that Jn that event the Govern- 
ment would have to appoint dele- 
tates for several provinces, ' where 
the elections cannot be held in time, 


The proposal to postpone the meet- 
int, for a year Is supported by, the 
argument that suMeient time would 
thus be made available for the com- 
pletion of the elections. 


The proposal for an indefinite 
postponement is based on the theory 
that the Central Kuomintang will 
then be in a position to convene the 
Conuress ut any time it deems con- 
venjent nnd necessary. 


The Commiltce, which is to report 
to the Central Executive Committee 
of the Kuomintang for final decison, 
Includes Mr, Chu Cheng, (President 
of the Judiclal Yuan), Gencral 
Chiang Tso-pin, -(Minister of 
Interior Affairs), ‘Mr. Yeh Chu- 
tsang, (Sccretary-General of the 
Kuomintang), Mr. Chen Li-fu 
(member of the Central Executive 
Committee of the Kuomintang), Mr. 
Wu Tze-hui, (veteran leader of the 
Kuomintang) and others. 

According to, the original pro- 
gramme of the Central -Kuomintany 
the object of the Congress is to mark 
the handing over by the Kuomintang 
to the people of its political power, 
when nv constitutional “Government 


will ‘replace the present one party | 


government.—Reuter.

Russia Starts - 
Counting. 
170,000,000 | 


Moseow, Sept. 30. 


ia now goin on for a great 


nacional census of the whole of the 
Soviet Union, . 


The enormous seale of the cen- 
January 6 is 
will be 
required: ; ; 

2.200,000 cnumcriators and 120,000 

“mstructory.” 

Over 1,000 tons of paper for the

85,000,000 census in 30

. sults, g 
“Calculating machine factories are

for summarising the results, Cerlsus 
bureaux haye been established In all 
republics, regions and towns, 

. ALREADY BEGUN 

Tho census has already begun in 
certain northern districts, which arc 
practically: cut off, from the world 
during the winter. : 

it has actually been completed in 
the Kanino-Timaon,, and Leshukov: 
districts, in Matochkin Shar, ‘Yugor 
Shar, jn the settlements of Krasino, 
Russanovo und Beluzhya Guba. ‘ 

Particulars of nbout 40,000 people, 
including over 1,000. nomads, have 
been recorded, 

The. enumerators. had to overcome 
tremendous diMeuitics, forcing thelr 
way through forcats and the © vast 
expanses of the trans-polar tundra. 

In many regions the preparations 
for the census reveal new settle- 
metnis ond a great iricrease of .the 
population, says the Tass Agency: 

In Petrozivodsk, centre of Karelia, 
the population ‘has doubled in ten 
years, and now renches 64,000. ;

eame to Hongkong und found her

ber 9 on’ the pretence that she wos

EXPEDITION 


- RESEARCH SHIP 
TO ANTARCTIC © 


London, Oct. 15," 

qt ts announced that the Royal 
Research ship William Scoresby will 
leave St. Katherine Dock, London, 
ina few days’ time to continue her 
whale-marking operations {nthe 
Antarctic, She will touch at Dakar 


“dnd Capetown and will then proceed 


south-enst to the ice edge in the 
vicinity. of Enderby Land, working 
on this and other whaling grounds 
ws-the-distribution-of-whales.renders— 
sdesirable. She will not touch Innd 
ugain until the end of the whaling 
season in March. ee 


During her last marking commis~ 
sion a year ago, some 700 whales 
were marked from the Willlam 
Scoresby. It is possible to regain. 
marks from operations of previous 
scarons and thus to gather informa- 
{lon not only on the migrations of 


whales, but on the question whether 
whales return to the same ground 
{in the south yenr after ycar—

TOO CUMBERSOME 


The impatience felt In. some | 


quarters over the slowness of 
the international committee 
method, as applied to the non- 
intervention agreement in the 
Spanish crisis, raises an issue of 
the utmost importance. It is a 
matter -which has frequently 
come” into prominence. in the 
troublous times of the past few 
years in many parts of the 
world. The failuro.of tho 
League of Nations to prevent the 
virtual absorption of Manchuria 
by Japan is an outstanding 
example of the injury 4 country 
can suffer whilat an internation- 
al body is discussing the pros 
and cons of a crisis. Long 
before the Lytton Commission 
was despatched, Japan's object 
had been achieved, and despite 
the fact that the Commissioners 
found that Japan's action was 
indefensible, the stark fact re- 
mains that China has been bereft 
of an immense area of territory

““PLT- SHAFT” 
MODELS 


‘During the past season 
we have-been carrying out 


extensive’ tests with 
frames constructed on a 
still more advanced 
principle, which is only 
made ‘possible ‘by the - 
“EULCRUM" process of 
building trames in block | 
form. Briefly, this new 
frame has no handle slips 
at all. Every joint is 
‘parallel, to. the line of oo 
play, with the result that exceptional resiliency and | 
extra drive are obtained from the shaft. Under 
the normal construction the shaft is, of course, 
locked by the haridle slips and the shock of impact 
is largely taken by the shoulders of the frames and, 
+ -of course, the gut, In our “'Pli-shaft’’ construction 
however, the shock is absorbed by the whole of the. . 
~ shaft as well,-with ‘the result that not only are the 4 
shoulders talways the weakest part-of the-racket) - 
relieved of some’ of the. strain, but ‘additional - 
aswell, Experience is rapidly 
leading-us to:concluda that the design of a racket: 
along these tines. represents one. of the greatest, 
advances which has ever taken place in the 
evolution of the tennis racket. ~~

Ethiopian dispute. Italy was 
declared the aggressor, but

+revent the annexation of a

bership of the League of

ing the same in the 
ease of the -Spanish civil war. 
A most imposing list of nations 
has agreed not to intervene in the 
conflict, but specific charges have 
been made that the agreement is 
net being observed. Under the 
rules of procedure agreed upon, 
the Non-Intervention Commitice 
will only listen to such charges 
as are submitted by a2 Govern- 
ment which is party to the 
agreement. It is then the duty 
of the Committee to examine the 
complaints with a-view_ to as- 
certaining the facts. In this 
instance, Russia, a signatory of 
the agreement, hae levelled 
specific charges against Portugal

DESCRIBES PROCESS

___ TURNS_TO_RAW_SUGAR____ 
Roughly, the process as perfected 
in Germany involves the hydrolysis 
or wntering of wood scraps, by treat- 
ing’ them with hydrochloric _ acid. 
After treatment they ure dried and 
treated agaln until the mixture be- 
comes a raw sugar. At this stage the 
wood ts already “food,” and while 


unfit for human consumption, is ex- |. 


cellent os’ fodder for hogs. The 
change Into food fit for human con- 
sumption requirea only the addition 
of two more manufacturing steps, 
“Thus itis possible to produce 
practlenlly oll the fyndamental ele- 
ments of nutrition from waste wood,”

ties of wood substances are wasted 
In the forest itself, Lesy than $0 per

timber while the rest is lost: Addi- 
‘tlonal wastes occur In sawmills and 
similar Industries,” :

things as food and’ oll have not at-

America os they have in Europe.

Europe that have no ofl of their own 
and a Umited food supply, the situa-

OLYMPIA MOTOR 
EXHIBITION 


FEATURES OF THE NEW 
. MODELS 


» London, Oct. 15. 
Closed curs predominate ut the 
Olympia, where the 30th Inter- 
natlonal Motor Exhibition opened to~ 
day with exhibits of 30 British and 
24 foreign manufactures. . 


Cars are becoming larger, with ow 
tendency towards Increased power. 
Freak stream-Uning is not seen, but 
the bodywork of most cars is clear- 
Yned, with receptactes for spare 
wheels and‘ luggage lockers incor- 
porated in the coachwork— Ventila- 
tion Is better on nearly all models, 
and independent suspension of wheels 
is becoming more common. 


Another noticeable improvement is 
car maintenance, which is ogoin 
much , simpler. Centralised chussis 
lubrication Is n feature on many of 
this year’s cars, and built-in four- 
Wheel Jacking systems ore now com- 
mon.— British Wireless.

Evidence of the excellent work 
being done by the Socicty of St. 
Vincent de Paul amongst the poor of 
the Colony, . irrespective of race, 
nationality or creed, is contained in 
the annual report, just Issticd. 


Accompanying the report fs an 
appeal to the gencrosity of the public 
on the occasion of the Al Fresco 
Fete being held on a vacant piece 
of land near the Rosary Church, 
Kowloon, on November. Ist, and on 
“Our Poor Day” strect sale of roscs 
on December Ist. 


the report shows that during the 
-slx months, January to June, this 
year, the Society contributed to the 
maintenance of 135 famllics, com- 
prising 216 persons, to whom assist- 
ance was given, while 733 visits were 
made to the poor in their -homes and 
76 visits in the Hospitals during the 
period under review. The disburse- 
ment for the whole of 1935 amount- 
ed, to $7,816.65, For the first six 
months of 1936 St was $4,264.50 ns 
againet $3,719.60 for the samo period 
in 1935.* 

The Society has also assisted the 
children of these poor people with 
shoes ond clothing. ‘The expendi- 
ture under this head in 1935 -was 
$220.00 while for six months ended 
June 1936 it amounted .to §86.75.

442 DEAD | IN 
“STORMS: 


i Manila, Oct. 16, 

It is officially announced that 442 
persons lost their lives in the recent 
typhoon andthe floods which follow- 
ced it. ‘ 

However, it is believed that o5 ao 
result of the floods cholern ‘and 
dyséntery epidemics will take a 
heavy toll, . ; 

Senor Manuel Quezon, the Pre- 
sident, and high officials, have made 
an extensive tour of the devastated 
areas In North Luzan provinces. 

In a speech in Cabanatuan, Senor 
Quezon promised unstinted govern- 
mental relief” to the aMicted com- 
munities.—United Press. 


“13 Killed In 
Bombay Riots 


TROOPS PATROLLING 
CITY STREETS 


Bombay, Oct, 15. 

Climaxing « duy of rioting .in 
which thirteen were killed and: 140 
wounded, Hindus and. Moslems 
fought'n pitched baltle in the centre 
of the city to-day. 

The trouble commenced when 
Hindus started to bulld'a temple in 
the Byculla district, which is densely 
populated with Moslems, who stoncd 
workmen. Thereafter the fighting 
spread throughout the city, 

Three police officers and five con- 
stables were injured. One officer, 
surrounded by rioters, in the Bhendy 
Bazaar, was forced to fire in self-} 
defence : 

Mosicms attacked stray Hindus in 
many parts of the city and troops 


were patrolling the strects at the enc J- 


of the day.—United Press.
